## Introduction1 / 引言

===Original===
"The book is not a novel" is a phrase often in the mouth of critics, who on second thoughts might, perhaps, add with less emphasis, "It does not conform to the common type of novel." Fortified, however, with that sense of rectitude that dictates conformity to our neighbours and a safe acquiescence in the mysterious movements of public taste, the critics have exclaimed with touching unanimity﻿—"What a pity Jefferies tried to write novels! Why didn't he stick to essays in natural history!"

===Chinese===
"这部作品算不上小说"——这是批评家们嘴边常挂的一句话，若再三思量，他们或许会换一种不那么绝对的说法："它不符合小说的常见类型。"然而，裹挟着那股要求从众、对公众趣味的神秘动向安之若素的"正直感"，批评家们以令人动容的齐声感叹道——"杰弗里斯（Richard Jefferies）居然写起了小说，真是可惜！他为什么不老老实实写他的博物随笔呢！"

===Original===
What a pity Jefferies should have given us *Amaryllis at the Fair*, and *After London*!﻿—this opinion has been propagated with such fervency that it seems almost a pity to disturb it by inquiring into the nature of these his achievements. Certainly the critics, and their critical echoes, are united. "He wrote some later novels of indifferent merit," says a critic in *Chambers' Encyclopædia*. "Has anyone ever been able to write with free and genuine appreciation of even the later novels?" asks or echoes a lady, Miss Grace Toplis, writing on Jefferies. "In brief, he was an essayist and not a novelist at all," says Mr. Henry Salt. "It is therefore certain that his importance for posterity will dwindle, if it has not already dwindled, to that given by a bundle of descriptive selections. But these will occupy a foremost place on their particular shelf, the shelf at the head of which stands Gilbert White and Gray," says Mr. George Saintsbury. "He was a reporter of genius, and he never got beyond reporting. Mr. Besant has the vitalising imagination which Jefferies lacked," says Mr. Henley in his review of Walter Besant's *Eulogy of Richard Jefferies*; and again, "They are not novels as he (Walter Besant) admits, they are a series of pictures.﻿ ﻿… That is the way he takes Jefferies at Jefferies' worst." Yes, it is very touching this unanimity, and it is therefore a pleasure for this critic to say that in his judgment *Amaryllis at the Fair* is one of the very few later-day novels of English country life that are worth putting on one's shelf, and that to make room for it he would turn out certain highly-praised novels by Hardy which do not ring quite true, novels which the critics and the public, again with touching unanimity, have voted to be of high rank. But what is a novel? the reader may ask. A novel, says the learned Charles Annandale, is "a fictitious prose narrative, involving some plot of greater or less intricacy, and professing to give a picture of real life, generally exhibiting the passions and sentiments, in a state of great activity, and especially the passion of love." Well, *Amaryllis at the Fair* is a fictitious prose narrative professing to give a picture of real life, and involving a plot of little intricacy. Certainly it exhibits the passions and sentiments in a state of great activity. But Mr. Henry Salt, whose little book on Jefferies is the best yet published, further remarks: "Jefferies was quite unable to give any vivid dramatic life to his stories﻿ ﻿… his instinct was that of the naturalist who observes and moralizes rather than that of the novelist who penetrates and interprets; and consequently his rustic characters, though strongly and clearly drawn, do not live, as, for example, those of Thomas Hardy live.﻿ ﻿… Men and animals are alike mere figures in his landscapes."

===Chinese===
杰弗里斯竟给我们留下了《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》和《伦敦之后》——多么可惜啊！这种论调传播得如此热切，以至于去探究这两部作品的真正价值反倒显得煞风景。批评家们和他们的回声们倒是众口一词。《钱伯斯百科全书》中的一位批评家说："他后来的几部小说水准平平。"格蕾丝·托普利斯（Grace Toplis）女士在论及杰弗里斯时反问道，或者说附和道："有人曾真正自由而真诚地欣赏过他后来的小说吗？"亨利·索尔特（Henry Salt）先生则一言以蔽之："简言之，他是散文家，根本不是小说家。"乔治·圣茨伯里（George Saintsbury）先生说："因此，他的重要性对于后世来说必将缩水——如果还没有缩水的话——缩水到一堆描写性选段所具有的地位。但这些选段将在它们那一格书架上占据首要位置，而那格书架的最前面站着吉尔伯特·怀特（Gilbert White）和格雷（Gray）。"威廉·恩利（William Henley）先生在评沃尔特·贝赞特（Walter Besant）的《理查德·杰弗里斯礼赞》时说："他是一位天才的记者，却始终未能超越新闻报道。贝赞特先生拥有杰弗里斯所缺乏的赋予生命活力的想象力。"他又说："正如他（沃尔特·贝赞特）所承认的，那些不是小说，而是一系列画面。……他抓住的是杰弗里斯最糟的一面。"是的，这种齐声附和的确令人动容，因此本评论者很乐意说出自己的判断：《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》是近年来英国乡村小说中极少数值得收入书架的作品之一，为了给它腾出位置，他情愿把某些备受赞誉的哈代小说请出去——那些作品并不那么真切，然而批评家们和公众又一次以令人动容的齐声将它们评为上乘之作。但小说究竟是什么呢？读者或许会问。博学的查尔斯·安南代尔（Charles Annandale）说，小说是"一种虚构的散文叙事，涉及或繁或简的某些情节，旨在描绘现实生活，通常展现处于高度活跃状态的情感与情绪，尤其是爱情"。那么，《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》确实是一种虚构的散文叙事，旨在描绘现实生活，情节并不复杂。它确实展现了处于高度活跃状态的情感与情绪。但亨利·索尔特先生——他那本关于杰弗里斯的小册子是目前最好的一部——进一步指出："杰弗里斯完全无法赋予他的故事以生动的戏剧性生命……他的本能是博物学家式的观察与说教，而非小说家式的洞察与诠释；因此他笔下的乡村人物虽然刻画得刚劲清晰，却不具有生命力——不像托马斯·哈代笔下的人物那样活灵活现。……人与动物在他笔下同样只是风景中的点缀。"

===Original===
So far the critics. Jefferies being justly held to be "no ordinary novelist," it is inferred by most that something is wrong with *Amaryllis at the Fair*, and the book has been passed over in silence. But we do not judge every novel by the same test. We do not judge *Tristram Shandy*, for example, by its intricate plot, or by its "vivid drama," we judge it simply as an artistic revelation of human life and by its humorous insight into human character. And judged by the same simple test *Amaryllis at the Fair*, we contend, is a living picture of life, a creative work of imagination of a high order. Iden, the unsuccessful farmer who "built for all time, and not for the circumstances of the hour," is a masterly piece of character drawing. But Iden is a personal portrait, the reader may object, Well, what about Uncle Toby? From what void did he spring? Iden, to our mind, is almost as masterly a conception, as broadly human a figure as Uncle Toby. And Mrs. Iden, where will you find this type of nervous, irritable wife, full of spiteful disillusioned love for her dilatory husband better painted than by Jefferies? But Mrs. Iden is a type, not an individual, the reader may say. Excellent reader! and what about the Widow Wadman? She is no less and no more of an individual than is Mrs. Iden. It was a great feat of Sterne to create so cunningly the atmosphere of the Shandy household, but Jefferies has accomplished an artistic feat also in drawing the relations of the Idens, father, mother, and daughter. How true, how unerringly true to human nature is this picture of the Iden household; how delicately felt and rendered to a hair is his picture of the father's sluggish, masculine will, pricked ineffectually by the waspish tongue of feminine criticism. Further, we not only have the family's idiosyncrasies, their habits, mental atmosphere, and domestic story brought before us in a hundred pages, easily and instinctively by the hand of the artist, but we have the whole book steeped in the breath of English spring, the restless ache of spring that thrills through the nerves, and stirs the sluggish winter blood; we have the spring feeling breaking from the March heavens and the March earth in copse, meadow, and ploughland, as it has scarcely been rendered before by English novelist. The description of Amaryllis running out into the March wind to call her father from his potato planting to see the daffodil; the picture of Iden pretending to sleep in his chair that he may watch the mice; the description of the girl Amaryllis watching the crowd of plain, ugly men of the countryside flocking along the road to the fair; the description of Amadis the invalid, in the old farm kitchen among the stalwart country folk﻿—all these pictures and a dozen others in the book are painted with a masterly hand. Pictures! the critical reader may complain. Yes, pictures of living men and women. What does it matter whether a revelation of human life is conveyed to us by pictures or by action so long as it is conveyed? Mr. Saintsbury classes Jefferies with Gray, presumably because both writers have written of the English landscape. With Gray! Jefferies in his work as a naturalist and observer of wild life may be classed merely for convenience with Gilbert White. But this classification only applies to one half of Jefferies' books. By his *Wild Life in a Southern County* he stands beside Gilbert White; by his *Story of My Heart* he stands by himself, a little apart from the poets, and by *Amaryllis at the Fair* he stands among the half-dozen country writers of the century whose work is racy of the English soil and of rural English human nature. We will name three of these writers, Barnes, Cobbett, Waugh, and our attentive readers can name the other three.

===Chinese===
以上便是批评家们的高见。既然杰弗里斯被公认为"非同寻常的小说家"，大多数人便推测《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》一定有问题，这本书也就被悄悄搁置了。但我们并非用同一把尺子衡量每一部小说。比如，我们不会用情节的曲折或"生动的戏剧性"来评判《项狄传》，我们只把它当作对人类生活的一种艺术性揭示，凭其对人性的幽默洞察来评价它。用同样简单的标准来衡量《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》，我们坚信，它是一幅鲜活的生活画卷，一件高度想象力的创作。艾登（Iden）——那位"为永恒而建造，而非为眼前时势而建造"的失败农夫——是一幅精湛的人物素描。但读者或许会反驳：艾登只是一个个人肖像；那么，托比叔叔（Uncle Toby）呢？他从何处虚空中蹦出来？在我们看来，艾登几乎与托比叔叔一样是一个大师级的构思，一个同样具有普遍人性的形象。至于艾登太太（Mrs. Iden）——你到哪里去找比杰弗里斯画得更好的那种神经质、易怒、对拖沓丈夫满怀着刻薄而幻灭之爱的妻子类型呢？但读者或许会说，艾登太太只是一个类型，不是个体。好读者啊！那寡妇沃德曼（Widow Wadman）呢？她作为个体的存在感并不比艾登太太多一分或少一分。斯特恩（Sterne）能如此巧妙地营造桑迪家庭的氛围，这是一大杰作；而杰弗里斯在描绘艾登一家人——父亲、母亲和女儿——的关系时，同样完成了一项艺术壮举。艾登家庭的这幅画，对人性的描绘是多么真实，多么丝毫不差；他对父亲那迟缓的男性意志的刻画，以及那女性批评的毒舌如何徒劳地刺痛它的描写，是多么精妙入微、纤毫毕现。不仅如此，这家人的癖性、习惯、精神氛围和家庭故事在一百页篇幅中轻松而本能地展现在我们面前——这是艺术家的手笔；整本书都浸润在英国春天的气息里，那春天令人焦躁的隐痛穿透神经，搅动冬季凝滞的血液；我们感受到春天的气息从三月的天空和三月的大地中迸发出来，在树丛、草地和犁过的田野里弥漫——这样的春意，英国小说家几乎从未如此描绘过。阿玛丽莉斯冲进三月的风中，唤她种土豆的父亲来看水仙花；艾登假装在椅子上睡着，其实是在看老鼠；少女阿玛丽莉斯注视着那群相貌平平、其貌不扬的乡下男人沿路涌向集市；病号阿玛迪斯（Amadis）待在老农舍的厨房里，四周是健壮的乡民——书中诸如此类的十余幅画面，皆是大师手笔。画面！挑剔的读者或许会抱怨。是的，活生生的男人和女人的画面。只要人类生活的启示传达给了我们，通过画面还是通过行动又有什么关系呢？圣茨伯里先生把杰弗里斯与格雷归为一类，大概是因为两位作家都写过英国风景。和格雷归为一类！杰弗里斯作为博物学家和野生动物观察者的作品，为方便起见可以和吉尔伯特·怀特归为一类。但这一分类只适用于杰弗里斯一半的著作。凭《南部郡的野生生活》（*Wild Life in a Southern County*），他与吉尔伯特·怀特比肩；凭《我心灵的故事》（*Story of My Heart*），他自成一家，略与诗人们疏离；凭《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》，他跻身本世纪五六位最具英国乡土气息、最能反映英国乡村人性的乡村作家之列。我们列出其中三位——巴恩斯（Barnes）、科贝特（Cobbett）、沃（Waugh），其余三位，细心的读者自能补全。

===Original===
To come back to *Amaryllis at the Fair*, why is it so masterly, or, further, wherein is it so masterly, the curious reader may inquire? "Is it not full of digressions? Granted that the first half of the 'novel' is beautiful in style, does not Jefferies suddenly break his method, introduce his own personality, intersperse abrupt disquisitions on food, illness, and Fleet Street? Is not that description of Iden's dinner a little﻿—well, a little unusual? In short, is not the book a disquisition on life from the standpoint of Jefferies' personal experiences? And if this is so, how can the book be so fine an achievement?" Oh, candid reader, with the voice of authority sounding in your ears (and have we not Mr. Henley and Mr. Saintsbury bound in critical amity against us), a book may break the formal rules, and yet it may yield to us just that salt of life which we may seek for vainly in the works of more faultless writers. The strength of *Amaryllis at the Fair* is that its beauty springs naturally from the prosaic earthly facts of life it narrates, and that, in the natural atmosphere breathed by its people, the prose and the poetry of their life are one. In the respect of the artistic naturalness of its homely picture, the book is very superior to, say *The Mayor of Casterbridge*, where we are conscious that the author has been at work arranging and rearranging his charming studies and impressions of the old-world people of Casterbridge into the pattern of an exciting plot. Now it is precisely in the artificed dramatic story of *The Mayor of Casterbridge*﻿—and we cite this novel as characteristic, both in its strength and weakness, of its distinguished author﻿—that we are brought to feel that we have not been shown the characters of Casterbridge going their way in life naturally, but that they have been moved about, kaleidoscopically, to suit the exigencies of the plot, and that the more this is so the less significance for us have their thoughts and actions. Watching the quick whirling changes of Farfrae and Lucetta, Henchard and Newson in the matrimonial mazes of the story, and listening to the chorus of the rustics in the wings, we perceive indeed whence comes that atmosphere of stage crisis and stage effect which suddenly introduces a disillusioning sense of unreality, and mars the artistic unity of this charming picture, so truthful in other respects to English rural life. Plot is Mr. Hardy's weakness, and perfect indeed and convincing would have been his pictures, if he could have thrown his plots and his rustic choruses to the four winds. May we not be thankful, therefore, that Jefferies was no hand at elaborating a plot, and that in *Amaryllis at the Fair*, the scenes, the descriptions, the conversations are spontaneous as life, and that Jefferies' commentary on them is like Fielding's commentary, a medium by which he lives with his characters. The author's imagination, memory, and instinctive perception are, indeed, all working together; and so his picture of human life in *Amaryllis* brings with it as convincing and as fresh a breath of life as we find in Cobbett's, Waugh's and Barnes' country writings. When a writer arrives at being perfectly natural in his atmosphere, his style and his subject seem to become one. He moves easily and surely. Out of the splintered mass of ideas and emotions, out of the sensations, the observations and revelations of his youth, and the atmosphere familiar to him through long feeling, he builds up a subtle and cunning picture for us, a complete illusion of life more true than the reality. For what prosaic people call the reality is merely the coordination in their own minds of perhaps a thousandth part of aspects of the life around them; and only this thousandth part they have noticed. But the creative mind builds up a living picture out of the thousands of aspects most of us are congenitally blind to. This is what Jefferies has done in *Amaryllis at the Fair*. The book is rich in the contradictory forces of life, in its quick twists and turns: we feel in it there is nature working alike in the leaves of grass outside the Idens' house, in the blustering winds round the walls, and in the minds of the characters indoors; and the style has the freshness of the April wind. Everything is growing, changing, breathing in the book. But the accomplished critics do not notice these trivial strengths. It is enough for them that Jefferies was not a novelist! Indeed, Mr. Saintsbury apparently thinks that Jefferies made a mistake in drawing his philosophy from an open-air study of nature, for he writes: "Unfortunately for Jefferies his philosophic background was not like Wordsworth's clear and cheerful, but wholly vague and partly gloomy." It was neither vague nor gloomy, we may remark, parenthetically, but we may admit that Jefferies saw too deeply into nature's workings, and had too sensuous a joy in life to interpret all Nature's doings, à la Wordsworth, and lend them a portentously moral significance.

===Chinese===
回到《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》——好奇的读者或许会问，它为什么如此高明，或者说，它究竟高明在哪里？"难道不是充满了离题之笔吗？即便承认这部'小说'的前半部文笔优美，杰弗里斯不也突然改变了笔法，引入了自己的个性，插进关于食物、疾病和舰队街（Fleet Street）的突兀议论吗？艾登晚餐那段描写难道不有点——嗯，有点反常吗？简言之，这本书难道不是从杰弗里斯个人经验出发的一篇人生论说吗？若果真如此，这本书怎么还能成为如此精妙的成就？"噢，坦率的读者啊，权威的声音在你耳畔回荡（恩利先生和圣茨伯里先生不是已经联手在批评界向我们发难了吗），一本书可以打破成规，却仍能给予我们在更完美无瑕的作品中苦苦寻觅而不得的生命的真味。《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》的力度在于，它的美自然地从它所叙述的平凡尘世事实中生长出来，在于书中人物呼吸着的自然氛围里，他们生活中的散文与诗歌合而为一。在其朴素画面的艺术性自然方面，这本书远胜于——比如说——《卡斯特桥市长》（*The Mayor of Casterbridge*），在后者中我们隐约感到作者一直在辛勤地将他对卡斯特桥旧日人物的迷人研究和印象重新排列组合，以适配一个引人入胜的情节模式。正是在《卡斯特桥市长》精心编排的戏剧性故事中——我们援引这部小说作为其杰出作者优缺点的典型代表——我们不禁感到，卡斯特桥的人物并非自然而然地走着他们的人生之路，而是为了迎合情节的需要被万花筒般地搬来搬去，而越是如此，他们的思想和行动对我们来说就越显得无关紧要。看着法尔弗雷（Farfrae）与卢塞塔（Lucetta）、亨查德（Henchard）与纽森（Newson）在婚姻迷宫中的急速旋转，听着侧翼乡民合唱队的伴唱，我们确实感受到了那种舞台危机和舞台效果的氛围从何而来——它突然带来一种幻灭的虚幻感，损害了这幅迷人画卷的艺术统一性——而这幅画在其他方面对英国乡村生活的描绘是如此真实。情节是哈代先生的弱点；如果他能将那些情节和乡民合唱队抛向四方，他的画面本该是完美无缺、令人信服的。那么，我们难道不该庆幸杰弗里斯不擅长编织情节吗？在《集市中的阿玛丽莉斯》中，场景、描写、对话都如生活般自然天成，杰弗里斯对它们的评论就像菲尔丁（Fielding）的评论，是他在与笔下人物共同生活的一种媒介。作者的想象力、记忆力和直觉感知确实都在协同运作；因此他在《阿玛丽莉斯》中对人类生活的描绘，带给我们的是与科贝特、沃和巴恩斯的乡村著作中同样令人信服、同样鲜活的生活气息。当一位作家在其氛围中达到完全自然的境界时，他的风格与题材便仿佛合而为一。他从容而稳健地运笔。从纷繁破碎的情感与思绪中，从青年时代的感受、观察与启示中，从长久浸润而熟悉于心的氛围中，他为我们构建了一幅微妙而精巧的画面，一种比现实更真实的生活幻象。因为那些庸常之人所谓的现实，不过是在他们自己头脑中对周围生活千分之一侧面的整合；而他们只注意到了这千分之一。但富有创造力的心灵能从我们大多数人天生视而不见的成千上万面中，构建出一幅活生生的画面。这正是杰弗里斯在《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》中所做到的。这本书充满了生命中矛盾的力量，充满了急剧的转折：我们能感受到自然在艾登家屋外的草叶中、在四壁周围的狂风中、在室内人物的心灵中，以同样的方式运作；而文风则具有四月微风的清新。书中万物都在生长、变化、呼吸。但那些功成名就的批评家们并未留意这些"微不足道"的优点。对他们来说，杰弗里斯不是小说家就够了！确实，圣茨伯里先生显然认为杰弗里斯从户外对自然的研究中汲取哲学是犯了错误，他写道："不幸的是，杰弗里斯的哲学背景不像华兹华斯那样明朗愉悦，而是完全模糊、部分阴郁的。"我们附带说一句，它既不模糊也不阴郁，但我们或许可以承认，杰弗里斯对自然运作的洞察过于深刻，对生命的感官之乐过于沉迷，以至于无法像华兹华斯那样诠释自然的一切行为，并赋予它们一种庄严的道德意义。

===Original===
The one charge that may with truth be brought against *Amaryllis at the Fair* is that its digressions damage the artistic illusion of the whole. The book shows the carelessness, the haste, the roughness of a sketch, a sketch, moreover, which Jefferies was not destined to carry to the end he had planned, but we repeat, let us be thankful that its artistic weaknesses are those of a sketch direct from nature, rather than those of an ambitious studio picture. And these digressions are an integral part of the book's character, just as the face of a man has its own blemishes: they are one with the spirit of the whole, and so, if they break somewhat the illusion of the scenes, they do not damage its spiritual unity. It is this spiritual unity on which we must insist, because *Amaryllis* is indeed Jefferies' last and complete testament on human life. He wrote it, or rather dictated it to his wife, as he lay in pain, slowly dying, and he has put into it the frankness of a dying man. How real, how solid, how deliciously sweet seemed those simple earthly joys, those human appetites of healthy, vigorous men to him! how intense is his passion and spiritual hunger for the beauty of earth! Like a flame shooting up from the log it is consuming, so this passion for the green earth, for the earth in wind and rain and sunshine, consumes the wasted, consumptive body of the dying man. The reality, the solidity of the homely farmhouse life he describes spring from the intensity with which he clings to all he loves, the cold March wind buffeting the face, the mating cries of the birds in the hot spring sunshine. Life is so terribly strong, so deliciously real, so full of man's unsatisfied hungry ache for happiness; and sweet is the craving, bitter the knowledge of the unfulfilment. So, inspiring and vivifying the whole, in every line of *Amaryllis* is Jefferies' philosophy of life. Jefferies "did not understand human nature," say the accomplished critics. Did he not? *Amaryllis at the Fair* is one of the truest criticisms of human life, oh reader, you are likely to meet with. The mixedness of things, the old, old human muddle, the meanness and stupidity and shortsightedness of humanity, the good salty taste of life in the healthy mouth, the spirituality of love, the strong earthy roots of appetite, man's lust of life, with circumstances awry, and the sharp wind blowing alike on the just and the unjust﻿—all is there on the printed page of *Amaryllis at the Fair*. The song of the wind and the roar of London unite and mingle therein for those who do not bring the exacting eye of superiority to this most human book.

===Chinese===
对《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》唯一可以据实提出的指责是：它的离题之笔损害了整体的艺术幻觉。这本书呈现出一种速写的随意、仓促和粗犷，而且这幅速写杰弗里斯未能按计划完成，但我们再说一遍，让我们庆幸的是，它的艺术缺陷是一幅直接来自自然的速写的缺陷，而非一幅雄心勃勃的画室作品的缺陷。而这些离题之笔正是本书性格的组成部分，正如一张脸自有其瑕疵：它们与全书的精神浑然一体，因此即便它们多少打破了场景的幻觉，也并不损害其精神上的统一性。我们必须强调的正是这种精神统一性，因为《阿玛丽莉斯》确实是杰弗里斯关于人类生活的最后、最完整的遗嘱。他写下它——或者说，口述给他的妻子——在他卧病在床、慢慢走向死亡的时候，他将一个垂死之人的坦诚注入其中。对他来说，那些朴素的尘世欢乐、那些健康而有活力的人的世俗欲望，是多么真实、多么坚实、多么甘美！他对大地之美的热爱和精神饥渴是多么强烈！就像从正在燃烧的木柴中窜出的火焰，这种对绿色大地、对风雨阳光下的大地的激情，消耗着这位垂死之人衰弱的、患了肺病的躯体。他所描绘的朴素农舍生活的真实与坚实，源于他紧紧依恋他所爱之一切的那份执念——那迎面扑来的寒冷三月风、那阳光炽热的春天里鸟儿的求偶啼鸣。生命是如此强健得可怕，如此甘美得真实，如此充满了人类未获满足的、饥渴的幸福之痛；渴望是甜蜜的，而无法实现的认知是苦涩的。于是，在《阿玛丽莉斯》的每一行文字中，都激荡着杰弗里斯的人生哲学，它激励并赋予全书以生命。批评家们说杰弗里斯"不懂人性"。他不懂吗？噢，读者啊，《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》是你所能遇到的对人类生活最真实的批评之一。事物的混杂、人类那古老的混乱、人性的卑劣、愚蠢和短视、健康嘴中生活的好咸味、爱的精神性、欲望的坚实大地根基、人类对生命的渴望、错位的际遇，以及那无分善恶同样吹拂的冷风——这一切都在《集市上的阿玛丽莉斯》的书页中。风之歌与伦敦之吼在其中汇合交融——献给那些不以高高在上的苛刻目光来读这本最富人性之书的人。

===Original===
Edward Garnett.

===Chinese===
爱德华·加尼特（Edward Garnett）。

===Original===
To

 Charles Prestwich Scott.

===Chinese===

谨献

 查尔斯·普雷斯维奇·斯科特（Charles Prestwich Scott）。

## Epigraph / 题词

===Original===
> "Our day is but a finger: bring large cups."
> — Alcæus

===Chinese===

> — 阿尔凯奥斯（Alcæus）

## I / I

===Original===
Amaryllis found the first daffodil flowering by the damask rose, and immediately ran to call her father to come and see it.
There are no damask roses now, like there used to be in summer at Coombe Oaks. I have never seen one since I last gathered one from that very bush. There are many grand roses, but no fragrance﻿—the fragrance is gone out of life. Instinctively as I pass gardens in summer I look under the shade of the trees for the old roses, but they are not to be found. The dreary nurseries of evergreens and laurels﻿—cemeteries they should be called, cemeteries in appearance and cemeteries of taste﻿—are innocent of such roses. They show you an acre of what they call roses growing out of dirty straw, spindly things with a knob on the top, which even dew can hardly sweeten. “No call for damask roses﻿—wouldn’t pay to grow they. Single they was, I thinks. No good. These be cut every morning and fetched by the flower-girls for gents’ buttonholes and ladies’ jackets. You won’t get no damask roses; they be died out.”
I think in despite of the nurseryman, or cemetery-keeper, that with patience I could get a damask rose even now by inquiring about from farmhouse to farmhouse. In time some old farmer, with a good old taste for old roses and pinks, would send me one; I have half a mind to try. But, alas! it is no use, I have nowhere to put it; I rent a house which is built in first-rate modern style, though small, of course, and there is a “garden” to it, but no place to put a damask rose. No place, because it is not “home,” and I cannot plant except round “home.” The plot or “patch” the landlord calls “the garden”﻿—it is about as wide as the border round a patch, old style﻿—is quite vacant, bare, and contains nothing but mould. It is nothing to me, and I cannot plant it.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯（Amaryllis）发现大马士革玫瑰旁的第一株水仙花开了，立刻跑去叫父亲来看。
如今再没有大马士革玫瑰了，不像从前夏天在库姆橡树园（Coombe Oaks）那样。自从我最后一次从那丛玫瑰上摘下一朵，就再也没见过。如今有许多华丽的玫瑰，却没有芬芳——芬芳已从生活中消逝。夏日经过花园，我总下意识地在树荫下寻找旧时的玫瑰，却再也找不到了。那些阴沉沉的常青植物和月桂苗圃——该叫它们墓地才对，外观是墓地，品味也是墓地——根本不会有那样的玫瑰。他们让你看一英亩所谓的玫瑰，从肮脏的稻草堆里长出来，细弱的植株顶着个疙瘩，连露水都难以让它们甜美起来。"谁要大马士革玫瑰——种它划不来。它们是单瓣的，我看。没用。这些花每天早上剪了，让卖花女拿去给先生们别在扣眼上，给女士们别在外套上。你们找不到大马士革玫瑰的；它们已经绝种了。"
我想，尽管苗圃老板——或者说墓地看守——这么说，只要我有耐心，挨家挨户地问，现在也能找到一株大马士革玫瑰。时间久了，总会有一个老农，凭着对老玫瑰和石竹的好品味，送我一株；我真有心试试。可是，唉！没用，我无处可种；我租的房子是上等现代风格的，虽然当然不大，还有个"花园"，却没有地方种大马士革玫瑰。没有地方，因为这不是"家"，而我除了"家"周围，别处不肯种。房东称为"花园"的那块地，或者"地块"——大约只有旧式地块边缘那么宽——空空荡荡，光秃秃的，除了泥土什么也没有。它与我无关，我没法在上面种花。

===Original===
Not only are there no damask roses, but there is no place for them nowadays, no “home,” only villas and rented houses. Anything rented in a town can never be “home.”
Farms that were practically taken on a hundred and twenty, or fifty, or perhaps two hundred years’ leases were “homes.” Consequently they had damask roses, bees, and birds about them.

===Chinese===
如今不仅没有大马士革玫瑰，连种它们的地方也没有了，没有"家"，只有别墅和出租屋。在镇上租来的任何东西，永远成不了"家"。
那些实际上按一百二十年、五十年，或者也许两百年租约经营的农场，才是"家"。所以它们周围才有大马士革玫瑰、蜜蜂和鸟雀。

===Original===
There had been daffodils in that spot at least a century, opening every March to the dry winds that shrivel up the brown dead leaves of winter, and carry them out from the bushes under the trees, sending them across the meadow﻿—fleeing like a routed army before the bayonets of the East. Every spring for a century at least the daffodils had bloomed there.

===Chinese===
那个地方的水仙花至少已生长了一个世纪，每年三月迎着干燥的风开放，那风将冬天枯黄的死叶吹皱，把它们从树下的灌木丛中卷出，送过草地——像一支溃败的军队在东方的刺刀前奔逃。一个世纪以来，至少每个春天，水仙花都在那里盛开。

===Original===
Amaryllis did not stay to think of the century, but ran round the corner of the house, and came face to face with the east wind, which took her with such force as to momentarily stay her progress. Her skirts were blown out horizontally, her ankles were exposed, and the front line of her shape (beginning to bud like spring) was sketched against the red brick wall. She laughed, but the strong gille filled her throat as if a hand had been thrust down it; the wind got its edge like a knife under her eyelids, between them and the eyeballs, and seemed as if it would scoop them out; her eyes were wet with involuntary tears; her lips dried up and parched in a moment. The wind went through her thick stockings as if the wool was nothing. She lifted her hand to defend her eyes, and the skin of her arm became “goosey” directly. Had she worn hat or bonnet it would have flown. Stooping forwards, she pushed step by step, and gradually reached the shelter of the high garden wall; there she could stand upright, and breathe again.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯没空去想那个世纪，她绕过屋角，迎面撞上东风，风力之猛，让她一时寸步难行。她的裙摆被吹得水平展开，脚踝露了出来，她身形的前缘（正像春天一样开始萌发）映在红砖墙上，勾勒出轮廓。她笑了，但猛烈的疾风灌满她的喉咙，仿佛有只手插了进去；风像刀子一样钻到她的上下眼皮之间，眼珠和眼皮之间，好像要把眼珠挖出来；她的眼睛不由自主地涌出泪水；嘴唇瞬间干裂了。风穿透她厚实的长袜，仿佛羊毛根本不存在。她抬手护住眼睛，手臂上的皮肤立刻起了鸡皮疙瘩。要是她戴着帽子或软帽，准会被吹飞。她弓着身子，一步一步往前挪，终于来到高高的花园墙下避风的地方；在那里她才能直起身子，重新呼吸。

===Original===
Her lips, which had been whitened by the keen blast, as if a storm of ice particles had been driven against them, now resumed their scarlet, but her ears were full of dust and reddened, and her curly dark hair was dry and rough and without gloss. Each separate hair separated itself from the next, and would not lie smooth﻿—the natural unctuous essence which usually caused them to adhere was dried up.
The wind had blown thus round that corner every March for a century, and in no degree abated its bitter force because a beautiful human child, full of the happiness of a flower, came carelessly into its power. Nothing ever shows the least consideration for human creatures.
The moss on the ridge of the wall under which she stood to breathe looked shrivelled and thin, the green tint dried out of it. A sparrow with a straw tried hard to reach the eaves of the house to put it in his nest, but the depending straw was caught by the breeze as a sail, and carried him past.

===Chinese===
她的嘴唇刚才被刺骨的寒风吹得发白，仿佛被一阵冰粒风暴打过，现在又恢复了红润，但她的耳朵里满是灰尘，红通通的，她卷曲的深色头发干枯粗糙，没有光泽。每一根头发都各自散开，不肯服帖——使它们通常黏结在一起的那种天然油润精华已被吹干。
一百年来，每年三月，风都这样绕过那个角落吹来，从未因一个美丽的孩童，满怀着花朵般的幸福，无意中闯入它的势力范围而减弱半分它的酷烈。大自然从不给人类丝毫怜悯。
她站着喘气的墙头上，苔藓看起来干瘪稀薄，绿色已被吹干。一只麻雀衔着一根稻草，拼命想够到屋檐，把草放进巢里，但垂下的稻草被微风当帆一样兜住，将它吹了过去。

===Original===
Under the wall was a large patch recently dug, beside the patch a grass path, and on the path a wheelbarrow. A man was busy putting in potatoes; he wore the raggedest coat ever seen on a respectable back. As the wind lifted the tails it was apparent that the lining was loose and only hung by threads, the cuffs were worn through, there was a hole beneath each arm, and on each shoulder the nap of the cloth was gone; the colour, which had once been grey, was now a mixture of several soils and numerous kinds of grit. The hat he had on was no better; it might have been made of some hard pasteboard, it was so bare. Every now and then the wind brought a few handfuls of dust over the wall from the road, and dropped it on his stooping back.
The way in which he was planting potatoes was wonderful, every potato was placed at exactly the right distance apart, and a hole made for it in the general trench; before it was set it was looked at and turned over, and the thumb rubbed against it to be sure that it was sound, and when finally put in, a little mould was delicately adjusted round to keep it in its right position till the whole row was buried. He carried the potatoes in his coat pocket﻿—those, that is, for the row﻿—and took them out one by one; had he been planting his own children he could not have been more careful. The science, the skill, and the experience brought to this potato-planting you would hardly credit; for all this care was founded upon observation, and arose from very large abilities on the part of the planter, though directed to so humble a purpose at that moment.

===Chinese===
墙下是一大片新翻的地，旁边是一条草径，草径上放着一辆独轮车。一个人正忙着种土豆；他穿着一件体面人身上从未见过的最破烂的外套。风掀起衣摆，可以看出衬里松了，只靠几根线挂着，袖口磨穿了，两个腋下各有个洞，两个肩膀上的绒毛都磨掉了；那颜色，原本是灰的，现在成了多种泥土和沙砾的混合物。他戴的帽子也好不到哪去；可能是什么硬纸板做的，光秃秃的。不时有风从路那边卷起几把灰尘，越过墙头，落在他弯着的背上。
他种土豆的方式令人称奇，每一颗土豆都以完全正确的间距放置，在总体沟垄中为它挖好坑；放下之前，他要看看，翻过来，用拇指搓一搓，确保它完好无损，最后放入时，还要细致地在周围培上一点土，让它保持在正确的位置，直到整行埋好。他把土豆装在外套口袋里——也就是这一行用的——一颗颗取出来；就算种的是他自己的孩子，也不会比这更仔细。你简直无法相信，这小小的土豆种植竟凝聚了科学、技巧和经验；因为所有这些细致的工作都基于观察，源于种植者极强的能力，尽管此刻那能力只用在如此卑微的目标上。

===Original===
So soon as Amaryllis had recovered breath, she ran down the grass path and stood by the wheelbarrow, but although her shadow fell across the potato row, he would not see her.
“Pa,” she said, not very loud. “Pa,” growing bolder. “Do come﻿—there’s a daffodil out, the very, very first.”
“Oh,” a sound like a growl﻿—“oh,” from the depth of a vast chest heaving out a doubtful note.
“It is such a beautiful colour!”
“Where is your mother?” looking at her askance and still stooping.
“Indoors﻿—at least﻿—I think﻿—no﻿—”
“Haven’t you got no sewing? Can’t you help her? What good be you on?”
“But this is such a lovely daffodil, and the very first﻿—now do come!”
“Flowers bean’t no use on; such trumpery as that; what do’ee want a-messing about arter thaay? You’ll never be no good on; you ain’t never got a apron on.”
“But﻿—just a minute now.”
“Go on in, and be some use on.”
Amaryllis’ lip fell; she turned and walked slowly away along the path, her head drooping forward.
Did ever anyone have a beautiful idea or feeling without being repulsed?

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯刚喘过气来，就沿着草径跑过去，站在独轮车旁，但尽管她的影子落在土豆行上，他还是不肯看她。
"爸，"她轻声说。"爸，"声音大了些。"快来——有一株水仙开了，第一株，第一株呢。"
"哦，"一声像低吼的声音——"哦，"从宽阔胸膛深处发出一个含糊的音。
"颜色真美啊！"
"你妈在哪？"他斜眼看着她，仍弯着腰。
"在屋里——至少——我想——不——"
"你没活干吗？不能帮帮她？你有什么用？"
"可这是多可爱的水仙花啊，而且是第一株——快来看看吧！"
"花有什么用；那种没用的东西；你干嘛要去摆弄那些玩意儿？你永远没出息；连条围裙都没系上。"
"可是——就一分钟。"
"进去，干点有用的活。"
阿玛丽莉斯的嘴唇垂了下来；她转身沿着小路慢慢走开，头向前耷拉着。
可曾有人怀抱着美好的念想或情感而不被拒绝？

===Original===
She had not reached the end of the path, however, when the father began to change his attitude; he stood up, dropped his “dibbler,” scraped his foot on his spade, and, grumbling to himself, went after her. She did not see or hear him till he overtook her.
“Please, I’ll go and do the sewing,” she said.
“Where be this yer flower?” gruffly.
“I’ll show you,” taking his ragged arm, and brightening up immediately. “Only think, to open in all this wind, and so cold﻿—isn’t it beautiful? It’s much more beautiful than the flowers that come in the summer.”
“Trumpery rubbish﻿—mean to dig ’em all up﻿—would if I had time,” muttered the father. “Have ’em carted out and drowed away﻿—do for ashes to drow on the fields. Never no good on to nobody, thaay thengs. You can’t eat ’em, can you, like you can potatoes?”
“But it’s lovely. Here it is,” and Amaryllis stepped on the patch tenderly, and lifted up the drooping face of the flower.
“Ah, yes,” said Iden, putting his left hand to his chin, a habit of his when thinking, and suddenly quite altering his pronunciation from that of the country folk and labourers amongst whom he dwelt to the correct accent of education. “Ah, yes; the daffodil was your great-uncle’s favourite flower.”

===Chinese===
然而，她还没走到小路尽头，父亲就开始改变态度了；他站起身，丢下"点穴器"，脚在铲子上蹭了蹭，一边嘀咕着，一边跟上了她。她没看见也没听见，直到他追上来。
"求你了，我去缝衣服，"她说。
"花在哪儿？"他粗声问道。
"我带你去，"她挽起他破烂的胳膊，立刻高兴起来。"你想想，在这么大的风里，这么冷的天开放——美不美？它比夏天开的花美多了。"
"没用的破玩意儿——我真想全挖了——有时间的话，"父亲嘟囔着。"让车拉出去扔了——正好当灰撒在田里。对谁都没用，那些东西。你不能吃它们，对吧？不像土豆能吃。"
"可它真美。就在这儿，"阿玛丽莉斯温柔地踏上菜地，轻轻托起那朵低垂的花。
"啊，是的，"艾登（Iden）把左手放在下巴上，这是他思考时的习惯，口音突然从他生活的乡民和劳工的腔调，变成了有教养的正确发音。"啊，是的；水仙是你曾叔公最喜欢的花。"

===Original===
“Richard?” asked Amaryllis.
“Richard,” repeated Iden. And Amaryllis, noting how handsome her father’s intellectual face looked, wandered in her mind from the flower as he talked, and marvelled how he could be so rough sometimes, and why he talked like the labourers, and wore a ragged coat﻿—he who was so full of wisdom in his other moods, and spoke, and thought, and indeed acted as a perfect gentleman.
“Richard’s favourite flower,” he went on. “He brought the daffodils down from Luckett’s; every one in the garden came from there. He was always reading poetry, and writing, and sketching, and yet he was such a capital man of business; no one could understand that. He built the mill, and saved heaps of money; he bought back the old place at Luckett’s, which belonged to us before Queen Elizabeth’s days; indeed, he very nearly made up the fortunes Nicholas and the rest of them got rid of. He was, indeed, a man. And now it is all going again﻿—faster than he made it. He used to take you on his knee and say you would walk well, because you had a good ankle.”

===Chinese===
"理查德（Richard）？"阿玛丽莉斯问道。
"理查德，"艾登重复道。阿玛丽莉斯注意到父亲睿智的面庞多么英俊，听着他说话，心思从花上飘远了，暗自惊叹他怎么有时会这么粗鲁，为什么说话像劳工，穿着破外套——而在他另一些情绪里，他却充满智慧，言谈举止都像个十足的绅士。
"理查德最喜欢的花，"他接着说。"他从勒基特（Luckett's）把水仙带下来；花园里的每一株都来自那儿。他总是在读诗，写作，素描，却又是个极出色的生意人；没人能理解这点。他建了磨坊，攒下大笔钱财；他买回了勒基特的旧宅，那是伊丽莎白女王时代之前就属于我们家的；说真的，他几乎弥补了尼古拉斯（Nicholas）和其他人挥霍掉的家产。他确实是个人物。如今一切又在流失——比他挣钱更快。他过去常把你抱在膝上，说你将来走路会很好看，因为你的脚踝很美。"

===Original===
Amaryllis blushed and smoothed her dress with her hands, as if that would lengthen the skirt and hide the ankles which Richard, the great-uncle, had admired when she was a child, being a man, but which her feminine acquaintances told her were heavy.
“Here, put on your hat and scarf; how foolish of you to go out in this wind without them!” said Mrs. Iden, coming out. She thrust them into Amaryllis’ unwilling hands, and retired indoors again immediately.
“He was the only one of all the family,” continued her father, “who could make money; all the rest could do nothing but spend it. For ten generations he was the only moneymaker and saver, and yet he was as free and liberal as possible. Very curious, wasn’t it?﻿—only one in ten generations﻿—difficult to understand why none of the others﻿—why﻿—” He paused, thinking.
Amaryllis, too, was silent, thinking﻿—thinking how easily her papa could make money, great heaps of money. She was sure he could if he tried, instead of planting potatoes.
“If only another Richard would rise up like him!” said Iden.
This was a very unreasonable wish, for, having had one genius in the family, and that, too, in the memory of man, they could not expect another. Even vast empires rarely produce more than one great man in all the course of their history. There was but one Caesar in the thousand years of Rome; Greece never had one as a nation, unless we except Themistocles, or unless we accept Alexander, who was a Macedonian; Persia had a Cyrus; there was a Tamerlane somewhere, but few people know anything of the empire he overshadows with his name; France has had two mighty warriors, Charlemagne and Napoleon﻿—unfortunate France! As for ourselves, fortunate islanders! we have never had a great man so immensely great as to overtop the whole, like Charlemagne in his day. Fortunate for us, indeed, that it has been so. But the best example to the point is the case of the immense empire of Russia, which has had one Peter the Great, and one only. Great-uncle Richard was the Peter the Great of his family, whose work had been slowly undone by his successors.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯脸红了，用手抚平裙子，仿佛这样就能让裙摆变长，遮住曾叔公理查德在她小时候（那时他还是个男人）曾赞美的脚踝，而她的女性朋友们却说她的脚踝太粗了。
"来，把帽子和围巾戴上；不戴这些就出去吹风，你多傻啊！"艾登太太走出来，把帽子和围巾硬塞进阿玛丽莉斯不情愿的手里，立刻又回了屋。
"他是整个家族中唯一能挣钱的，"父亲继续说。"其余的人只会花钱。十代人里，他是唯一的挣钱者和储蓄者，但他又极其慷慨大方。非常奇怪，不是吗？——十代人里只有一个——难以理解为什么其他人没有——为什么——"他停住了，陷入沉思。
阿玛丽莉斯也沉默了，想着——想着她爸爸要是愿意，挣钱是多么轻而易举，能挣大把大把的钱。她相信他只要尝试，而不是种土豆，就一定能做到。
"但愿再出一个理查德那样的人！"艾登说。
这是一个很不切实际的愿望，因为家族中已经出过一个天才，而且还是在人们记忆犹新的时代，他们不能再指望另一个。即使幅员辽阔的帝国，在整个历史长河中也很少产生不止一个伟人。罗马千年历史中只有一个凯撒（Caesar）；希腊作为民族从未有过，除非我们不算忒弥斯托克利斯（Themistocles），或者除非我们算上亚历山大（Alexander），但他是个马其顿人；波斯有过一个居鲁士（Cyrus）；某个地方有个帖木儿（Tamerlane），但很少有人知道他那笼罩着他的名字的帝国；法国有过两位伟大的战士，查理曼（Charlemagne）和拿破仑（Napoleon）——不幸的法国！至于我们自己，幸运的岛民！我们从未有过一个像查理曼那样伟大到凌驾于一切之上的伟人。对我们来说，这确实是幸运的。但最贴切的例子是庞大的俄罗斯帝国，它有过一个彼得大帝（Peter the Great），仅此一个。曾叔公理查德就是他家族中的彼得大帝，他的基业被后来者慢慢败光了。

===Original===
“I wonder whether any of us will ever turn out like Richard,” continued Iden. “No one could deny him long; he had a way of persuading and convincing people, and always got his own will in the end. Wonderful man!” he pondered, returning towards his work.
Suddenly the side door opened, and Mrs. Iden just peered out, and cried, “Put your hat and scarf on directly.”
Amaryllis put the hat on, and wound the scarf very loosely about her neck. She accompanied her father to the potato patch, hoping that he would go on talking, but he was quickly absorbed in the potatoes. She watched him stooping till his back was an arch; in fact, he had stooped so much that now he could not stand upright, though still in the prime of life; if he stood up and stretched himself, still his back was bowed at the shoulders. He worked so hard﻿—ever since she could remember she had seen him working like this; he was up in the morning while it was yet dark tending the cattle; sometimes he was up all night with them, wind or weather made no difference. Other people stopped indoors if it rained much, but it made no difference to her father, nor did the deep snow or the sharp frosts. Always at work, and he could talk so cleverly, too, and knew everything, and yet they were so short of money. How could this be?
What a fallacy it is that hard work is the making of money; I could show you plenty of men who have worked the whole of their lives as hard as ever could possibly be, and who are still as far off independence as when they began. In fact, that is the rule; the winning of independence is rarely the result of work, else nine out of ten would be well-to-do.

===Chinese===
"不知我们中会不会有人变得像理查德那样，"艾登继续说。"没人能长久地否决他；他有办法说服人、让人信服，最终总能遂他的意。了不起的人！"他沉思着，回到他的活计旁。
突然，侧门开了，艾登太太探出头来，喊道："马上把帽子和围巾戴上。"
阿玛丽莉斯戴上帽子，把围巾非常松地绕在脖子上。她陪父亲走到土豆地，希望他接着说下去，但他很快就埋头于土豆了。她看着他弯腰弯成一张弓；事实上，他弯得太厉害了，以至于现在虽然正当壮年，却已经站不直了；即使他站直身子伸懒腰，肩膀处的背依然是弓着的。他干活太卖力了——从她记事起，就看他这样干活；天还没亮他就起床照料牲口；有时他整夜守着它们，不管刮风还是下雨。别人大雨天都待在家里，但这对她父亲没有区别，大雪和严霜也没有区别。他总是干活，而且他说话又那么聪明，什么都懂，可他们却这么缺钱。这是怎么回事？
"努力工作就能挣钱"是多么荒谬的谬论啊；我可以给你看许多人，他们一辈子都尽可能地拼命干活，结果离独立还和当初一样远。事实上，这才是常理；独立很少是工作的结果，否则十有八九都该是富裕人家了。

## II / II

===Original===
Presently Amaryllis wandered indoors, and was met in the hall by her mother.
“What has he been talking to you about?” she said, angrily. “Don’t listen to him. He will never do any good. Just look at his coat; it’s a disgrace, a positive disgrace. Telling you about the old people? What’s the use of talking of people who have been dead all this time? Why doesn’t he do something himself? Don’t listen to his rubbish﻿—wasting his time there with potatoes, it is enough to make one wild! Why doesn’t he go in to market and buy and sell cattle, and turn over money in that way? Not he! he’d rather muddle with a few paltry potatoes, as if it mattered an atom how they were stuck in the ground.”

===Chinese===
不一会儿，阿玛丽莉斯溜进屋里，在门厅被母亲拦住。
"他跟你说什么了？"她生气地说。"别听他的。他永远干不出什么名堂。看看他的外套；简直丢人，真丢人。跟你说那些老掉牙的人？说那些死了那么久的人有什么用？他自己为什么不做点事？别听他那套废话——在那儿摆弄几颗破土豆，真是让人发疯！他干嘛不去市场做买卖，买卖牲口，那样周转钱？他才不呢！他宁愿捣鼓几颗可怜的土豆，好像怎么把它们埋进地里有那么重要似的。"

===Original===
Not liking to hear her father abused, Amaryllis went upstairs, and when she was alone lifted her skirt and looked at the ankles which great-uncle Richard had admired. Other girls had told her they were thick, and she was ashamed of them.
Instead of the slender things which seem as if a sudden strain would snap them, and are nothing but mere bone, she had a pair of well-shaped ankles, justly proportioned to what would soon be a fine form; strong, but neither thick, nor coarse, nor heavy, ankles that would carry her many a mile without weariness, that ended good legs with plenty of flesh on them. The stupidity of calling such coarse or heavy! They were really ideal ankles, such as a sculptor would carve. Yet these ill-instructed girls called them coarse! It was not their fault, it was the lack of instruction; as they did not know what was physically perfect, of course they could not recognize it.
Let every girl who has such ankles be proud of them, for they will prove a blessing to her for the whole of her life.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯不喜欢听人贬低父亲，于是上了楼，独自一人时，掀起裙子看了看曾叔公理查德曾赞美的那双脚踝。别的女孩子曾说她的脚踝太粗，她为此感到羞愧。
她的脚踝并非那种似乎一用力就会折断的纤细骨头，而是一双形状优美、与她那即将出落成的好身段恰成比例的脚踝；结实，但既不粗，也不笨，也不重，那双脚踝能让她走上许多英里而不觉疲倦，末端是丰腴的好腿。说它们粗或重，是多么愚蠢！它们其实是雕塑家会雕刻的理想的脚踝。可那些没见识的女孩子竟说它们粗！这不是她们的错，是缺乏教导；她们不知道什么是形体上的完美，当然也就认不出来。
每个有这样的脚踝的女孩子都该为此自豪，因为它们将证明是她一生的福气。

===Original===
Amaryllis could not get her hair smooth, though she brushed it for some time; it would not lie close, so much had the east wind dried it. She opened a drawer, and took out a little bottle of macassar, and held it in her hand, balancing probabilities. Would her father see it if she used it, or might he, perhaps, fail to notice? She dared not leave the bottle on the dressing-table, for if he had chanced to pass through the room he would certainly have thrown it out of window, so bitter was his antagonism to all oils and perfumes, scents, pomades, and other resources of the hairdresser, which he held defiled the hair and ruined it, to the deception of woman and the disgust of man. Not one drop of scent did Amaryllis dare to sprinkle on her handkerchief, not one drop of oil did she dare put on her beautiful hair unless surreptitiously, and then she could not go near him, for he was certain to detect it and scorch her with withering satire.
Yet, however satirized, feminine faith in perfumes and oils and so forth is like a perennial spring, and never fails.
Such splendid hair as Amaryllis possessed needed no dressing﻿—nothing could possibly improve it, and the chances therefore were that whatever she used would injure﻿—yet in her heart she yearned to rub it with oil.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯花了好一阵子梳头，头发还是弄不顺；它不肯服帖，东风把它吹得太干了。她拉开抽屉，拿出一小瓶玛卡萨护发油，拿在手里，掂量着可能性。要是用了，父亲会看到吗？或许他可能不会注意到？她不敢把瓶子留在梳妆台上，因为他要是碰巧经过房间，准会把它扔出窗外，他极其厌恶一切油脂、香水、香料、发蜡和理发师的其他用具，认为它们玷污了头发，毁了它，欺骗了女人，让男人恶心。阿玛丽莉斯连一滴香水都不敢洒在手帕上，连一滴油都不敢往她美丽的头发上抹，除非偷偷地用，用完还不能走近他，因为他一定会闻出来，用刻薄的讽刺把她烤焦。
然而，无论被怎样讽刺，女性对香水、油脂等等的信仰就像一眼永不枯竭的清泉，从不衰竭。
阿玛丽莉斯那一头华丽的秀发本不需要打理——不可能有什么能改善它，所以无论她用什么都可能损伤它——但她内心深处渴望用油擦一擦。

===Original===
But the more she considered the more probable it seemed that her father would detect her; she had better wait till he went out for the afternoon somewhere, an event that seldom occurred, for Iden was one of those who preferred working at home to rambling abroad. He was, indeed, too attached to his home work. So she returned the bottle to the drawer, and hid it under some stockings.
Immediately afterwards it was dinnertime. At all meals the rule was that there must be no talking, but at dinner the law was so strict that even to ask for anything, as a piece of bread, or to say so much as “Give me the salt, please,” was a deadly sin. There must be absolute silence while the master ate. The least infringement was visited with a severe glance from his keen and brilliant blue eyes﻿—there are no eyes so stern as blue eyes when angry﻿—or else he uttered a deep sigh like a grunt, and sat rigidly upright for a moment. For he usually stooped, and to sit upright showed annoyance. No laws of the Medes and Persians were ever obeyed as was this law of silence in that house.

===Chinese===
但她越想越觉得父亲很可能发现；她最好等他下午出门去某个地方，这种事很少发生，因为艾登是那种宁愿在家干活也不愿出门闲逛的人。事实上，他太恋家了。于是她把瓶子放回抽屉，藏在几双袜子下面。
随后就是晚餐时间。所有用餐的规矩都是不许说话，但晚餐的规矩严格到连要任何东西，比如一片面包，或者说一句"请把盐递给我"，都是死罪。主人吃饭时必须绝对安静。哪怕最轻微的违犯，也会招来他那双敏锐而明亮的蓝眼睛严厉的一瞥——没有比生气时的蓝眼睛更严厉的了——或者他会发出一声深沉的叹息，像哼了一声，然后挺直腰板坐一会儿。因为他通常是弯着腰的，挺直身子就表示不悦。米底人和波斯人的任何法律，都不如这宅子里的沉默法则被遵守得那么严格。

===Original===
Anything that disturbed the absolute calm of the dinner hour was worse than sacrilege; anything that threatened to disturb it was watched intently by that repressive eye. No one must come in or go out of the room; if anyone knocked at the door (there are no bells in old country houses) there was a frown immediately, it necessitated someone answering it, and then Mrs. Iden or Amaryllis had to leave the table, to go out and open and shut the sitting-room door as they went, and again as they returned. Amaryllis dreaded a knock at the door, it was so awful to have to stir once they had sat down to dinner, and the servant was certain not to know what reply to give. Sometimes it happened﻿—and this was very terrible﻿—that the master himself had to go, someone wanted him about some hay or a horse and cart, and no one could tell what to do but the master. A dinner broken up in this way was a very serious matter indeed.
That day they had a leg of mutton﻿—a special occasion﻿—a joint to be looked on reverently. Mr. Iden had walked into the town to choose it himself some days previously, and brought it home on foot in a flag basket. The butcher would have sent it, and if not, there were men on the farm who could have fetched it, but it was much too important to be left to a second person. No one could do it right but Mr. Iden himself.

===Chinese===
任何打扰晚餐时间绝对平静的事都比亵渎神灵更严重；任何可能打扰它的事，都受到那双压抑的眼睛的严密监视。不许有人进出房间；如果有人敲门（老式乡间宅子里没有门铃），立刻就会皱起眉头，因为这必然需要有人去应门，然后艾登太太或阿玛丽莉斯就得离席，出去开门，进门时关上起居室的门，回来时再关一次。阿玛丽莉斯害怕有人敲门，因为一旦在餐桌旁坐下，要起身是多么可怕的事，而且女仆肯定不知道该怎么回话。有时会发生——这非常可怕——主人自己不得不亲自去，因为有人找他处理干草或马车的事，除了主人没人能拿主意。一顿饭这样被打断，确实是非常严重的事。
那天他们吃的是羊腿——特殊场合——一块令人肃然起敬的肉。艾登先生几天前亲自进城挑选了它，提着柳条篮子步行带回家。肉店老板本可以送来，如果不行，农场上也有人能去取，但这事太重要了，不能交给第二个人。只有艾登先生自己才能做好。

===Original===
There was a good deal of reason in this personal care of the meat, for it is a certain fact that unless you do look after such things yourself, and that persistently, too, you never get it first-rate. For this cause people in grand villas scarcely ever have anything worth eating on their tables. Their household expenses reach thousands yearly, and yet they rarely have anything eatable, and their dinner-tables can never show meat, vegetables, or fruit equal to Mr. Iden’s. The meat was dark brown, as mutton should be, for if it is the least bit white it is sure to be poor; the grain was short, and ate like bread and butter, firm, and yet almost crumbling to the touch; it was full of juicy red gravy, and cut pleasantly, the knife went through it nicely; you can tell good meat directly you touch it with the knife. It was cooked to a turn, and had been done at a wood fire on a hearth; no oven taste, no taint of coal gas or carbon; the pure flame of wood had browned it. Such emanations as there may be from burning logs are odorous of the woodland, of the sunshine, of the fields and fresh air; the wood simply gives out as it burns the sweetness it has imbibed through its leaves from the atmosphere which floats above grass and flowers. Essences of this order, if they do penetrate the fibres of the meat, add to its flavour a delicate aroma. Grass-fed meat, cooked at a wood fire, for me.

===Chinese===
这种亲自照管肉是有充分道理的，因为事实确实如此：除非你亲自照管这类事，而且持之以恒，否则你永远吃不到上等的。正因为如此，那些豪华别墅里的人，餐桌上几乎从没有值得吃的东西。他们的家用每年高达数千，却很少有什么可吃的，他们的晚餐桌上永远拿不出比得上艾登先生家的肉、蔬菜或水果。肉是深褐色的，羊肉本该如此，因为哪怕稍微发白，就一定是劣等的；纹理细密，吃起来像面包和黄油，结实，但一碰几乎就碎了；满是鲜红的汁液，切起来很顺手，刀子切进去毫不费力；好肉一碰刀子就知道。它火候刚好，是在炉膛里的柴火上烧的；没有烤箱的味道，没有煤气或碳的污染；纯木的火焰把它烤得焦黄。燃烧的原木散发出的气息，带着林地的芬芳，带着阳光、田野和新鲜空气的味道；木头在燃烧时，只是释放出它通过叶子从飘浮在草和花上的空气中汲取的甜香。这类精髓，如果真的渗透到肉的纤维里，会给肉增添一种精致的香气。吃草养大的肉，在柴火上烹饪——这才是我的菜。

===Original===
Wonderful it is that wealthy people can endure to have their meat cooked over coal or in a shut-up iron box, where it kills itself with its own steam, which ought to escape. But then wealthy villa people do do odd things. *Les Misérables* who have to write like myself must put up with anything and be thankful for permission to exist; but people with mighty incomes from tea, or crockery-ware, or mud, or bricks and mortar﻿—why on earth these happy and favoured mortals do not live like the gods passes understanding.
Parisian people use charcoal: perhaps Paris will convert some of you who will not listen to a farmer.
Mr. Iden had himself grown the potatoes that were placed before him. They were white, floury, without a drop of water in the whole dish of them. They were equal to the finest bread﻿—far, far superior to the bread with which the immense city of London permits itself to be poisoned. (It is not much better, for it destroys the digestion.) This, too, with wheat at thirty shillings the quarter, a price which is in itself one of the most wonderful things of the age. The finest bread ought to be cheap.

===Chinese===
令人惊讶的是，富人居然能忍受肉在煤上烤，或者在封闭的铁盒子里烤，肉在里面被自己的蒸汽闷死，而蒸汽本该散掉。不过话说回来，那些住别墅的有钱人确实会做些怪事。像我这样的《悲惨世界》（Les Miserables）作者，必须忍受一切，并感激能活着；但那些靠茶叶、陶器、泥土、砖石砂浆赚大钱的人——为什么这些幸福而受宠的凡人不像神一样生活，实在令人费解。
巴黎人用木炭：也许巴黎能让你们中那些不肯听农民的话的人开开窍。
摆在他面前的土豆是艾登先生自己种的。土豆白净，粉糯，整盘里没有一滴水。它们堪比最好的面包——远远、远远超过那座庞大的伦敦城允许自己被毒害的面包。（它也好不了多少，因为它破坏消化。）而且，小麦还是三十先令一蒲式耳，这个价格本身就是这个时代最奇妙的事之一。最好的面包本该便宜。

===Original===
“They be forty-folds,” said Mr. Iden, helping himself to half a dozen. “Look at the gravy go up into um like tea up a knob of sugar.”
The gravy was drawn up among the dry, floury particles of the potatoes as if they had formed capillary tubes.
“Forty-folds,” he repeated; “they comes forty to one. It be an amazing theng how thengs do that; forty grows for one. Thaay be an old-fashioned potato; you won’t find many of thaay, not true forty-folds. Mine comes true, ’cause I saves um every year a’ purpose. Better take more than that (to Amaryllis)﻿—you haven’t got but two” (to Mrs. Iden).
What he ate other people at his table must eat, and the largest quantity possible. No one else must speak, hardly to say “Yes” or “No,” but the master could talk, talk, talk without end. The only talking that might be done by others was in praise of the edibles on the table by Iden so carefully provided. You might admire the potatoes or the mutton, but you must not talk on any other subject. Nor was it safe even to do that, because if you said, “What capital potatoes!” you were immediately helped to another plateful, and had to finish them, want them or not. If you praised the mutton several thick slices were placed on your plate, and woe to you if you left a particle. It was no use to try and cover over what you could not manage with knife and fork; it was sure to be seen. “What bean’t you going to yet (eat) up that there juicy bit, you?”
Amaryllis and Mrs. Iden, warned by previous experience, discreetly refrained from admiring either mutton or potatoes.

===Chinese===
"这是四十倍产量的，"艾登先生说着，给自己盛了六颗。"看汁水怎么渗进土豆里，就像茶渗进糖块一样。"
汁水被吸入土豆干燥的粉质颗粒中，仿佛形成了毛细管。
"四十倍，"他重复道；"四十颗收一颗。真是神奇的事，怎么会有这种事；一颗长四十颗。这是老式土豆；你找不到多少了，真正的四十倍品种。我的是正宗的，因为我每年特意留种。你该多吃点，"他对阿玛丽莉斯说——"你只吃了两颗，"他对艾登太太说。
他吃什么，同桌的人也必须吃什么，而且要尽可能多吃。其他人不许说话，连"是"或"不"都不能说，但主人可以滔滔不绝地说个没完。其他人唯一能说的话，就是赞美艾登先生精心准备的菜肴。你可以夸土豆或羊肉，但不能谈任何其他话题。即使这样也不保险，因为如果你说"多好的土豆啊！"你马上就会被再添一盘，不管你喜不喜欢都得吃完。如果你夸了羊肉，几大片厚肉就会放在你盘子里，要是剩下一丁点你就倒霉了。想把刀叉对付不了的东西盖住是没用的；准会被发现。"你怎么还不把那块多汁的吃掉？"
阿玛丽莉斯和艾登太太，吸取了以往的教训，谨慎地不去夸奖羊肉或土豆。

## III / 第III章

===Original===
“Forty-folds,” went on the master, “be the best keeping potatoes. Thur be so many new sorts now, but they bean’t no good; they be very good for gentlefolk as doan’t know no better, and poor folk as can’t help theirselves. They won’t grow everywhere neither; there bean’t but one patch in our garden as ull grow ’um well. It’s that’s big middle patch. Summat different in the soil thur. There’s a lot, bless you! to be learned before you can grow a potato, for all it looks such a simple thing. Farty-folds﻿—”

===Chinese===
“四十褶（Forty-folds），”艾登继续说道，“是最好的耐储存土豆。如今出了那么多新品种，可都不中用；只有那些不懂好歹的绅士淑女才会觉得它们好，穷苦人更是没法子挑拣。也不是哪儿都能种；咱家园子里就那一小块地能种好。就是中间那块大的。那里的土总归有些不同。种土豆学问大着呢，老天爷！看着简单，里头门道多得很。四十褶——”

===Original===
“Farty-folds!” said Mrs. Iden, imitating his provincial pronunciation with extreme disgust in her tone.

===Chinese===
“四十褶！”艾登夫人模仿他的乡土口音，语气里满是厌恶。

===Original===
“Aw, yes, too,” said Iden. “Varty-volds be ould potatoes, and thur bean’t none as can beat um.”

===Chinese===
“哎，是呢，”艾登说，“四十褶就是老品种土豆，没谁比得过它。”

===Original===
The more she showed her irritation at his speech or ways, the more he accentuated both language and manner.

===Chinese===
她越是表露出对他说话方式的恼怒，他就越是变本加厉地拿腔拿调。

===Original===
“Talking with your mouth full,” said Mrs. Iden. It was true, Iden did talk with his mouth full, very full indeed, for he fed heartily. The remark annoyed him; he grunted and spluttered and choked a little﻿—floury things are choky. He got it down by taking a long draught at his quart of strong ale. Splendid ale it was, too, the stuff to induce you to make faces at Goliath. He soon began to talk again.

===Chinese===
“满嘴饭说话，”艾登夫人说。这话不假，艾登确实满嘴饭说话，嘴塞得满满的，因为他吃得实在。这话让他恼了；他咕哝着、喷着饭粒、呛了几口——淀粉类的东西容易噎人。他灌下一大口夸脱装的浓啤酒，总算顺了下去。那啤酒确实了得，喝下去能叫你对着歌利亚（Goliath）做鬼脸。他很快又开始说起来。

===Original===
“Th’ ould shepherd fetched me these swede greens; I axed un three days ago; I know’d we was going to have this yer mutton. You got to settle these yer things aforehand.”

===Chinese===
“老牧羊人给我弄来这些瑞典芜菁叶；我三天前就跟他要了；我知道今儿要吃这羊肉。这些事得提前张罗好。”

===Original===
“Axed,” muttered Mrs. Iden.

===Chinese===
“要了，”艾登夫人嘟囔道。

===Original===
“Th’ pigeons have been at um, they be ’mazing fond of um, so be the larks. These be the best as thur was. They be the best things in the world for the blood. Swede greens be the top of all physic. If you can get fresh swede tops you don’t want a doctor within twenty miles. Their’s nothing in all the chemists’ shops in England equal to swede greens”﻿—helping himself to a large quantity of salt.

===Chinese===
“鸽子也来啄它们，可爱吃啦，云雀也一样。这些可是最好的。对血脉好得不能再好了。瑞典芜菁叶是顶好的药。你要是能弄到新鲜的芜菁尖，二十英里内都不用请医生。全英格兰药房里的东西都比不上瑞典芜菁叶。”他一边说一边往碗里舀了一大勺盐。

===Original===
“What a lot of salt you *do* eat!” muttered Mrs. Iden.

===Chinese===
“你可真能吃盐！”艾登夫人嘟囔道。

===Original===
“Onely you must have the real swedes﻿—not thuck stuff they sells in towns; greens they was once p’rhaps, but they be tough as leather, and haven’t got a drop of sap in um. Swedes is onely to be got about March.”

===Chinese===
“不过你得吃真的瑞典芜菁——可不是城里卖的那种；从前或许是嫩叶，可如今硬得跟皮革似的，里头一点汁水都没有。瑞典芜菁只有三月前后才有。”

===Original===
“Pooh! you can get them at Christmas in London,” said Mrs. Iden.

===Chinese===
“哼！伦敦圣诞节都能买到，”艾登夫人说。

===Original===
“Aw, can ’ee? Call they swede tops? They bean’t no good; you might as well eat dried leaves. I tell you these are the young fresh green shoots of spring”﻿—suddenly changing his pronunciation as he became interested in his subject and forgot the shafts of irritation shot at him by his wife. “They are full of sap﻿—fresh sap﻿—the juice which the plant extracts from the earth as the active power of the sun’s rays increases. It is this sap which is so good for the blood. Without it the vegetable is no more than a woody fibre. Why the sap should be so powerful I cannot tell you; no one knows, any more than they know *how* the plant prepares it. This is one of those things which defy analysis﻿—the laboratory is at fault, and can do nothing with it.” (“More salt!” muttered Mrs. Iden. “How can you eat such a quantity of salt?”) “There is something beyond what the laboratory can lay hands on; something that cannot be weighed, or seen, or estimated, neither by quantity, quality, or by any means. They analyse champagne, for instance; they find so many parts water, so much sugar, so much this, and so much that; but out of the hundred parts there remain ten﻿—I think it is ten﻿—at all events so many parts still to be accounted for. They escape, they are set down as volatile﻿—the laboratory has not even a distinct name for this component; the laboratory knows nothing at all about it, cannot even name it. But this unknown constituent is the real champagne. So it is with the sap. In spring the sap possesses a certain virtue; at other times of the year the leaf is still green, but useless to us.”

===Chinese===
“哎，真有？那也叫瑞典芜菁尖？不中用；跟吃干树叶没两样。我告诉你，这才是春天的嫩芽嫩叶。”他突然改了口音，话题一扯开就忘了妻子甩过来的冷言冷语。“这里面满是汁水——新鲜的汁水——就是植物趁着日头渐强从泥土里汲取的精华。这汁水对血脉好极了。没有它，蔬菜就只是一团粗纤维。为什么这汁水有这般神效，我说不上来；谁也不知道，就像谁也不知道植物*怎么*制出这汁水似的。这类事就是没法分析——实验室没辙，什么也做不了。”（“又加盐！”艾登夫人嘟囔道，“你怎么吃得了这么多盐？”）“有些东西是实验室够不着的；没法称重、没法看见、没法估量，不管从数量、质量还是什么法子都测不出来。就拿香槟来说吧；他们分析出多少水、多少糖、多少这个、多少那个；可一百份里头还剩十份——我想是十份——反正有好些份还没搞明白。那些成分溜走了，被归为挥发物——实验室连个确切的名字都叫不上来；实验室对它一窍不通，连名字都给不出。可正是这未知成分才是真正的香槟。汁水也是这个理儿。春天的汁水有某种功效；一年里其他时候叶子还是绿的，对我们却没用了。”

===Original===
“I shall have some vinegar,” said Mrs. Iden, defiantly, stretching out her hand to the cruet.

===Chinese===
“我要加点醋，”艾登夫人挑衅地伸出手去拿醋瓶。

===Original===
Mr. Iden made a wry face, as if the mere mention of vinegar had set his teeth on edge. He looked the other way and ate as fast as he could, to close his eyes to the spectacle of anyone spoiling the sappy swede greens with nauseous vinegar. To his system of edible philosophy vinegar was utterly antagonistic﻿—destructive of the sap-principle, altogether wrong, and, in fact, wicked, as destroying good and precious food.

===Chinese===
艾登做了个苦脸，好像光听人提醋就倒牙了似的。他别过脸去，拼命快吃，眼不见为净，不忍看有人拿恶心的醋糟蹋这汁水丰盈的瑞典芜菁叶。在他那套饮食哲学里，醋是头号大敌——破坏了汁水之道，大错特错，简直伤天害理，糟蹋了好东西。

===Original===
Amaryllis would not have dared to have taken the vinegar herself, but as her mother passed the cruet to her, she, too, fell away, and mixed vinegar with the green vegetables. All women like vinegar.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯（Amaryllis）本不敢自己拿醋，可母亲把醋瓶递给她，她也就随了大流，在菜里拌上了醋。女人都好这一口。

===Original===
When the bottle was restored to the cruet-stand Mr. Iden deigned to look round again at the table.

===Chinese===
醋瓶放回瓶架，艾登才屈尊又瞥了一眼饭桌。

===Original===
“Ha! you’ll cut your thumb!” he shouted to Amaryllis, who was cutting a piece of bread. She put the loaf down with a consciousness of guilt. “Haven’t I told you how to cut bread twenty times? Cutting towards your thumb like that! Hold your left hand lower down, so that if the knife slips it will go over. Here, like this. Give it to me.”

===Chinese===
“嘿！你要切到拇指了！”他冲阿玛丽莉斯吼道，她正切着面包。她心虚地放下面包。“我不是教过你二十回怎么切面包了吗？刀往拇指那边切！左手放低些，刀滑了才不会切到。来，这样。给我。”

===Original===
He cut a slice to show her, and then tossed the slice across the table so accurately that it fell exactly into its proper place by her plate. He had a habit of tossing things in that way.

===Chinese===
他切了一片给她看，然后把那片面包往桌上一抛，抛得极准，不偏不倚正好落在她盘子旁边的老位置。他有随手抛东西的习惯。

===Original===
“Why ever couldn’t you pass it on the tray?” said Mrs. Iden. “Flinging in that manner! I hate to see it.”

===Chinese===
“你用托盘递一下不成吗？”艾登夫人说，“这样甩来甩去！看着就烦。”

===Original===
Amaryllis, as in duty bound, in appearance took the lesson in bread-cutting to heart, as she had done twenty times before. But she knew she should still cut a loaf in the same dangerous style when out of his sight. She could not do it in the safe way﻿—it was so much easier in the other; and if she did cut her hand she did not greatly care.

===Chinese===
阿玛丽莉斯照例做出受教的样子，把切面包的训诫记在心里，就像前二十次一样。可她知道，只要趁他看不见，她还是会用那危险的法子切。安全的切法她做不来——另一种容易多了；况且就算切了手，她也不大在乎。

===Original===
“Now perhaps you’ll remember,” said the master, getting up with his plate in his hand.

===Chinese===
“这下你该记住了，”主人端着盘子站起来说。

===Original===
“Whatever *are* you going to do now?” asked Mrs. Iden, who knew perfectly well.

===Chinese===
“你*又要*干什么？”艾登夫人问道，心里其实门儿清。

===Original===
“Going to warm the plate.” He went out into the kitchen, sat down by the fire, and carefully warmed his plate for a second helping.

===Chinese===
“去把盘子烘热。”他走进厨房，在炉火旁坐下，仔仔细细地把盘子烘热，好盛第二份。

===Original===
“I should think you couldn’t want any more,” said Mrs. Iden when he came back. “You had enough the first time for three.”

===Chinese===
“我看你该吃不下了，”他回来时艾登夫人说，“你头一份够三个人吃的。”

===Original===
But Iden, who had the appetite of a giant, and had never ruined his digestion with vinegar or sauces, piled another series of thick slices on his plate, now hot to liquefy the gravy, and added to the meat a just proportion of vegetables. In proportion and a just mixture the secret of eating successfully consisted, according to him.

===Chinese===
可艾登胃口大如巨人，且从没让醋或酱汁毁了脾胃，又在盘子里摞了一层厚厚的肉片——盘子现在热了，能把肉汁化开——再配上恰到好处的蔬菜。在他看来，吃得好的秘诀就在于比例得当、搭配均匀。

===Original===
First he ate a piece of the dark brown mutton, this was immediately followed by a portion of floury potato, next by a portion of swede tops, and then, lest a too savoury taste should remain in the mouth, he took a fragment of bread, as it were to sweeten and cleanse his teeth. Finally came a draught of strong ale, and after a brief moment the same ingredients were mixed in the same order as before. His dinner was thus eaten in a certain order, and with a kind of rhythm, duly exciting each particular flavour like a rhyme in its proper position, and duly putting it out with its correct successor. Always the savour of meat and gravy and vegetables had to be toned down by the ultimate bread, a vast piece of which he kept beside him. He was a great bread eater﻿—it was always bread after everything, and if there were two courses then bread between to prepare the palate, and to prevent the sweets from quarrelling with the acids. Organization was the chief characteristic of his mind﻿—his very dinner was organized and well planned, and any break or disturbance was not so much an annoyance in itself as destructive of a clever design, like a stick thrust through the web of a geometrical spider.

===Chinese===
他先吃一块深褐色的羊肉，接着吃一口粉糯的土豆，再吃一口瑞典芜菁叶，然后唯恐嘴里留下太浓的味道，就掰一块面包，好比是清口净齿。最后灌一口浓啤酒，稍等片刻，又按原来的顺序把同样的食物往嘴里送。他吃这顿饭遵循一定的次序，带着某种韵律，每种味道都在恰当的位置被唤醒，好比诗行各就各位，又被继之而来的味道恰当地冲淡。肉味、汁味、菜味，始终要靠最后那块面包来调和，他手边总搁着一大块。他是个吃面包的大户——每样东西吃完都得来口面包；要是有两道菜，那中间也要插面包清清口，免得甜的跟酸的打架。有条理是他头脑最显著的特点——他吃顿饭都组织得井井有条，任何打断或扰乱本身倒不怎么烦人，却毁了一番巧妙的设计，好比一根棍子捅穿了几何蜘蛛的网。

===Original===
This order of mouthfuls had been explained over and over again to the family, and if they felt that he was in a more than usually terrible mood, and if they felt his gaze upon them, the family to some extent submitted. Neither Mrs. Iden nor Amaryllis, however, could ever educate their palates into this fixed sequence of feeding; and, if Iden was not in a very awful and Jovelike mood, they wandered about irregularly in their eating. When the dinner was over (and, indeed, before it began) they had a way of visiting the larder, and “picking” little fragments of pies, or cold fowl, even a cold potato, the smallest mug﻿—a quarter of a pint of the Goliath ale between them, or, if it was to be had, a sip of port wine. These women were very irrational in their feeding; they actually put vinegar on cold cabbage; they gloated over a fragment of pickled salmon about eleven o’clock in the morning. They had a herring sometimes for tea﻿—the smell of it cooking sent the master into fits of indignation, he abominated it so, but they were so hardened and lost to righteousness they always repeated the offence next time the itinerant fish-dealer called. You could not drum them into good solid, straightforward eating.

===Chinese===
这套吃法顺序他跟家人翻来覆去讲了多少遍，要是他们觉出他心情格外糟，要是感到他在盯着他们看，一家子多少会顺从些。可艾登夫人和阿玛丽莉斯的舌头怎么也教不会按固定顺序吃；只要艾登没有一副雷霆万钧、天神下凡的架势，她们吃起东西来就乱了套。饭一吃完（其实还没开饭她们就这样），她们总要去食品储藏室转转，"偷"点馅饼渣子、冷鸡肉，哪怕是冷土豆，再分喝一小杯——半品脱歌利亚啤酒，要是有酒的话，就抿一口波特酒。这母女俩吃东西全无章法；她们居然往冷卷心菜上浇醋；上午十一点来钟还对着一小块腌鲑鱼垂涎欲滴。有时候喝茶时还要来条鲱鱼——那鱼一下锅，主人就气得七窍生烟，他最厌恶这个，可她们脸皮厚了，忘了正道，下回鱼贩子一来照买不误。你没法把她们训成规规矩矩、老老实实吃饭的人。

===Original===
They generally had a smuggled bit of pastry to eat in the kitchen after dinner, for Mr. Iden considered that no one could need a second course after first-rate mutton and forty-folds. A morsel of cheese if you liked﻿—nothing more. In summer the great garden abounded with fruit; he would have nothing but rhubarb, rhubarb, rhubarb, day after day, or else black-currant pudding. He held that black currants were the most wholesome fruit that grew; if he fancied his hands were not quite clean he would rub them with black-currant leaves to give them a pleasant aromatic odour (as ladies use scented soap). He rubbed them with walnut-leaves for the same purpose.

===Chinese===
她们通常在饭后偷偷溜进厨房吃块偷藏的点心，因为艾登先生认为，吃完上好的羊肉和四十褶土豆，谁都不该再要第二道。想吃奶酪倒可以——仅此而已。夏天大花园里水果多得很，可他只要大黄，大黄，大黄，一天接一天，要么就是黑加仑布丁。他认为黑加仑是顶有益的水果；要是觉得手没洗干净，就拿黑加仑叶子搓一搓，好让手带上一股清香（就像女士们用香皂一样）。他也用核桃叶子搓手，道理一样。

===Original===
Of salad in its season he was a great eater, cucumber especially, and lettuce and celery; but a mixed salad (oil and a flash, as it were, of Worcester sauce) was a horror to him. A principle ran through all his eating﻿—an idea, a plan and design.

===Chinese===
到了吃沙拉的季节，他吃得很多，尤其好黄瓜、莴苣和芹菜；但拌沙拉——拌上油和几滴伍斯特酱——在他看来可怕得很。他吃什么都讲究一个原则——一套想法、一份计划、一番设计。

===Original===
I assure you it is a very important matter this eating, a man’s fortune depends on his dinner. I should have been as rich as Croesus if I could only have eaten what I liked all my time; I am sure I should, now I come to look back.

===Chinese===
我跟你说，吃这件事非常重要，一个人的造化全看他的晚饭。要是我这辈子都能想吃什么吃什么，我肯定像克罗伊斯（Croesus）一样有钱；如今回头看，我确信如此。

===Original===
The soundest and most wholesome food in the world was set on Mr. Iden’s table; you may differ from his system, but you would have enjoyed the dark brown mutton, the floury potatoes, the fresh vegetables and fruit and salad, and the Goliath ale.

===Chinese===
摆在艾登先生餐桌上的，是世上最地道、最有益的食物；你也许不认同他的理论，但那深褐色的羊肉、粉糯的土豆、新鲜的蔬果沙拉和歌利亚啤酒，你一定吃得开心。

===Original===
When he had at last finished his meal he took his knife and carefully scraped his crumbs together, drawing the edge along the cloth, first one way and then the other, till he had a little heap; for, eating so much bread, he made many crumbs. Having got them together, he proceeded to shovel them into his mouth with the end of his knife, so that not one was wasted. Sometimes he sprinkled a little moist sugar over them with his finger and thumb. He then cut himself a slice of bread and cheese, and sat down with it in his armchair by the fire, spreading his large red-and-yellow silk handkerchief on his knee to catch the fragments in lieu of a plate.

===Chinese===
吃完饭，他拿刀仔仔细细把面包渣刮到一起，刀刃沿着桌布来回划拉，直到攒了一小堆；他吃了那么多面包，碎渣自然不少。渣子拢好后，他就用刀尖往嘴里铲，一点不浪费。有时候他用拇指和食指捏点湿糖撒在上面。然后切一片面包奶酪，端着坐到炉边扶手椅上，把那方大红大黄的丝手帕铺在膝盖上代替盘子接碎渣。

===Original===
“Why can’t you eat your cheese at the table, like other people?” said Mrs. Iden, shuffling her feet with contemptuous annoyance. A deep grunt in the throat was the answer she received; at the same time he turned his armchair more towards the fire, as much as to say, “Other people are nothing to me.”

===Chinese===
“你就不能像别人一样在桌上吃奶酪吗？”艾登夫人说，轻蔑地挪了挪脚。她得到的回应是喉咙深处一声闷哼；同时他把扶手椅又转向炉火一些，意思是说："别人管我什么事。"

## IV / 第四章

===Original===
This armchair, of old-fashioned make, had lost an arm﻿—the screw remained sticking up, but the woodwork on that side was gone. It had been accidentally broken some ten years since; yet, although he used the chair every day, the arm had never been mended. Awkward as it was, he let it alone.
"Hum! where's *The Standard*, then?" he said presently, as he nibbled his cheese and sipped the ale which he had placed on the hob.
"Here it is, Pa," said Amaryllis, hastening with the paper.
"Thought you despised the papers?" said Mrs. Iden. "Thought there was nothing but lies and rubbish in them, according to you?"
"No more thur bean't."
"You always take good care to read them, though."
"Hum!" Another deep grunt, and another slight turn of the chair. He could not answer this charge of inconsistency, for it was a fact that he affected to despise the newspaper and yet read it with avidity, and would almost as soon have missed his ale as his news.

===Chinese===
这把扶手椅是老式样，断了一只扶手——螺丝还戳在那儿，但那半边的木头已经没了。大约十年前不小心弄坏的；然而尽管他天天坐这把椅子，那只扶手却始终没修。它虽然碍事，他却随它去。
"嗯！《旗帜报》（*The Standard*）呢？"他一边小口啃着奶酪、啜着搁在壁炉架上的麦芽酒，一边问道。
"给您，爸，"阿玛丽莉斯（Amaryllis）急忙拿着报纸跑过来。
"还以为你瞧不起报纸呢？"艾登太太（Mrs. Iden）说，"照你说的，报纸上不全是谎言和垃圾吗？"
"本来就是嘛。"
"可你总还是看得挺起劲的。"
"嗯！"又是一声深沉的嘟囔，椅子又轻轻转了一下。他无法反驳这种自相矛盾的指控，因为事实是他嘴上装着瞧不起报纸，读起来却如饥似渴，要是让他选择，漏了麦芽酒和漏了新闻一样让他难受。

===Original===
However, to settle with his conscience, he had a manner of holding the paper half aslant a good way from him, and every now and then as he read uttered a dissentient or disgusted grunt.
The master's taking up his paper was a signal for all other persons to leave the room, and not to return till he had finished his news and his nap.
Mrs. Iden and Amaryllis, as they went out, each took as many of the dishes as they could carry, for it was uncertain when they could come in again to clear the table. The cloth must not be moved, the door opened, or the slightest sound heard till the siesta was over.
"Can't clear the dinner things till four o'clock," said Mrs. Iden as she went, "and then you want your tea﻿—senseless!" Amaryllis shut the door, and the master was left to himself.

===Chinese===
不过，为了安抚自己的良心，他养成了把报纸拿得远远的、半斜着举的习惯，时不时在阅读时发出不满或厌恶的嘟囔声。
主人拿起报纸便是信号——其他人统统离开房间，等他看完新闻、打完盹再回来。
艾登太太和阿玛丽莉斯出门时，各自尽可能多地端走盘碟，因为谁也说不准什么时候才能再进来收拾桌子。桌布不能挪，门不能开，一点声响都不能有，午觉没醒之前一概不行。
"得到四点钟才能收拾碗碟，"艾登太太出门时说，"到时候你又要喝茶——没脑子！"阿玛丽莉斯关上门，主人便独自留下来了。

===Original===
By-and-by, his cheese being finished, he dropped his newspaper, and arranged himself for slumber. His left elbow he carefully fitted to the remnant of the broken woodwork of the chair. The silk handkerchief, red and yellow, he gathered into a loose pad in his left hand for his cheek and temple to rest on. His face was thus supported by his hand and arm, while the side of his head touched and rested against the wainscot of the wall.
Just where his head touched it the wainscot had been worn away by the daily pressure, leaving a round spot. The wood was there exposed﻿—a round spot, an inch or two in diameter, being completely bare of varnish. So many nods﻿—the attrition of thirty years and more of nodding﻿—had gradually ground away the coat with which the painter had originally covered the wood. It even looked a little hollow﻿—a little depressed﻿—as if his head had scooped out a shallow crater; but this was probably an illusion, the eye being deceived by the difference in colour between the wood and the varnish around it.

===Chinese===
过了一会儿，奶酪吃完了，他扔下报纸，调整姿势准备睡觉。他把左肘小心翼翼地搁在椅子断木的残茬上，将那条红黄相间的丝绸手帕松松地团成一个软垫，握在左手里垫着脸颊和太阳穴。他的脸就这样靠在手和臂上，头的一侧则触着、倚着墙上的护壁板。
他的头触着的那一处，护壁板被日复一日的挤压磨损了，留下一个圆形的印痕。木头裸露了出来——一个直径一两英寸的圆圈，上面的清漆已完全磨掉。无数次的点头——三十多年点头不止的磨蚀——渐渐磨掉了漆工最初涂在木头上的那层漆。它看起来甚至有点凹陷——有点塌下去——仿佛他的头在那里掏出一个浅浅的弹坑；但这大概是错觉，眼睛被那块木头与周围漆面之间的色差欺骗了。

===Original===
This human mark reminded one of the grooves worn by the knees of generations of worshippers in the sacred steps of the temple which they ascended on all-fours. It was, indeed, a mark of devotion, as Mrs. Iden and others, not very keen observers, would have said, to the god of Sleep; in truth, it was a singular instance of continued devotion at the throne of the god of Thought.
It was to think that Mr. Iden in the commencement assumed this posture of slumber, and commanded silence. But thought which has been cultivated for a third of a century is apt to tone down to something very near somnolence.
That panel of wainscot was, in fact, as worthy of preservation as those on which the early artists delineated the Madonna and Infant, and for which high prices are now paid. It was intensely﻿—superlatively﻿—human. Worn in slow time by a human head within which a great mind was working under the most unhappy conditions, it had the deep value attaching to inanimate things which have witnessed intolerable suffering.

===Chinese===
这道人留下的印记让人想起朝圣者世代的膝盖在圣殿台阶上跪拜磨出的沟痕——他们四肢着地爬上那些台阶。在艾登太太和那些不太敏锐的观察者看来，这确实是对睡神的一种虔诚标记；而实际上，它是在思想之神的宝座前持续虔诚的独特实例。
起初，艾登先生确实摆出这个假寐的姿态，要求保持安静。但经过三十余年修习的思维，往往会渐渐弱化为某种极近于昏睡的状态。
事实上，那块护壁板与早期艺术家描绘圣母与圣婴的那些画板一样值得保存——后者的售价如今已相当高昂。它是极其——登峰造极地——充满人性的。在缓慢的时光中，被一颗在最不幸的境遇下运转的伟大头脑磨蚀而成，它具有那种见证了难以忍受的痛苦的无生命之物所特有的深沉价值。

===Original===
I am not a Roman Catholic, but I must confess that if I could be assured any particular piece of wood had really formed a part of the Cross I should think it the most valuable thing in the world, to which Koh-i-noors would be mud.
I am a pagan, and think the heart and soul above crowns.
That panel was in effect a cross on which a heart had been tortured for the third of a century, that is, for the space of time allotted to a generation.
That mark upon the panel had still a further meaning, it represented the unhappiness, the misfortunes, the Nemesis of two hundred years. This family of Idens had endured already two hundred years of unhappiness and discordance for no original fault of theirs, simply because they had once been fortunate of old time, and therefore they had to work out that hour of sunshine to the utmost depths of shadow.
The panel of the wainscot upon which that mark had been worn was in effect a cross upon which a human heart had been tortured﻿—and thought can, indeed, torture﻿—for a third of a century. For Iden had learned to know himself, and despaired.

===Chinese===
我不是罗马天主教徒，但我必须承认，如果有人能向我保证某块木头确实曾是十字架的一部分，我会认为它是世界上最珍贵的东西，与之相比，"光明之山"（Koh-i-noor）钻石不过是泥土。
我是异教徒，认为心与灵魂高于王冠。
那块板实际上是一副十字架，一颗心灵在上面被折磨了整整三分之一世纪——也就是一代人被分配到的时间。
板上的那道印记还有更深层的含义，它代表着两百年的不幸、厄运与报应。艾登（Iden）这个家族已经承受了两百年的不幸与不和，而这一切并非出于他们自身的原初过错，只因为他们祖上曾经幸运过，因此他们必须将那一小时的阳光所换来的代价，一路偿付到最深的阴影里。
磨出那道印记的护壁板，实际上是一副十字架，一颗人心在上面被折磨了整整三分之一世纪——而思想确实能够折磨人。因为艾登已经学会了认识自己，于是绝望了。

===Original===
Not long after he had settled himself and closed his eyes the handle of the door was very softly turned, and Amaryllis stole in for her book, which she had forgotten. She succeeded in getting it on tiptoe without a sound, but in shutting the door the lock clicked, and she heard him kick the fender angrily with his iron-shod heel.
After that there was utter silence, except the ticking of the American clock﻿—a loud and distinct tick in the still (and in that sense vacant) room.
Presently a shadow somewhat darkened the window, a noiseless shadow; Mrs. Iden had come quietly round the house, and stood in the March wind, watching the sleeping man. She had a shawl about her shoulders﻿—she put out her clenched hand from under its folds, and shook her fist at him, muttering to herself, "Never *do* anything; nothing but sleep, sleep, sleep: talk, talk, talk; never *do* anything. That's what I hate."
The noiseless shadow disappeared; the common American clock continued its loud tick, tick.

===Chinese===
他安顿下来、闭上眼睛没多久，门把手被轻轻转动了，阿玛丽莉斯悄悄进来拿她忘拿的书。她踮着脚尖成功地拿到了书，没有弄出声响，但关门时锁头咔嗒响了一声，她听见他愤怒地用带铁掌的鞋跟踢了一脚壁炉围栏。
之后便是一片死寂，只有美国挂钟的滴答声——在这寂静（在这个意义上也就是空荡）的房间里，滴答声响亮而清晰。
不一会儿，一个影子微微遮暗了窗户，一个无声的影子；艾登太太悄悄绕到房子这边来，站在三月的风中，注视着这个睡着的男人。她肩上披着披肩——她从褶皱中伸出紧握的拳头，冲他挥了挥，嘴里嘟囔着："从来*不*做任何事；除了睡觉、睡觉、睡觉：说呀、说呀、说；从来*不*做任何事。我恨的就是这个。"
无声的影子消失了；那座普通的美国挂钟继续响亮地滴答、滴答。

===Original===
Slight sounds, faint rustlings, began to be audible among the cinders in the fender. The dry cinders were pushed about by something passing between them. After a while a brown mouse peered out at the end of the fender under Iden's chair, looked round a moment, and went back to the grate. In a minute he came again, and ventured somewhat farther across the width of the white hearthstone to the verge of the carpet. This advance was made step by step, but on reaching the carpet the mouse rushed home to cover in one run﻿—like children at "touch wood," going out from a place of safety very cautiously, returning swiftly. The next time another mouse followed, and a third appeared at the other end of the fender. By degrees they got under the table, and helped themselves to the crumbs; one mounted a chair and reached the cloth, but soon descended, afraid to stay there. Five or six mice were now busy at their dinner.
The sleeping man was as still and quiet as if carved.

===Chinese===
壁炉围栏里的煤渣中开始传来轻微的声响，微弱的沙沙声。有什么东西从干燥的煤渣间穿过，把它们拨来拨去。过了一会儿，一只棕色的老鼠从艾登椅子下的围栏尽头探出头来，四处张望了一下，又回到壁炉里。一分钟后它又来了，更大胆地穿过白色的壁炉石面，一直走到地毯边上。这番推进是一步一步来的，但一到地毯边，老鼠便飞快地跑回掩体——就像孩子们玩"摸树"游戏，从安全处小心翼翼地出去，再飞快地跑回来。接着另一只老鼠跟来了，第三只出现在围栏的另一头。渐渐地，它们钻到桌子底下，自顾自地吃起面包屑来；一只爬上椅子够到了桌布，但很快又溜下来，不敢久留。五六只老鼠现在正忙着吃它们的晚餐。
那个睡着的人一动不动、安安静静，仿佛一尊雕塑。

===Original===
A mouse came to the foot, clad in a great rusty-hued iron-shod boot﻿—the foot that rested on the fender, for he had crossed his knees. His ragged and dingy trouser, full of March dust, and earth-stained by labour, was drawn up somewhat higher than the boot. It took the mouse several trials to reach the trouser, but he succeeded, and audaciously mounted to Iden's knee. Another quickly followed, and there the pair of them feasted on the crumbs of bread and cheese caught in the folds of his trousers.
One great brown hand was in his pocket, close to them﻿—a mighty hand, beside which they were pygmies indeed in the land of the giants. What would have been the value of their lives between a finger and thumb that could crack a ripe and strong-shelled walnut?
The size﻿—the mass﻿—the weight of his hand alone was as a hill overshadowing them; his broad frame like the Alps; his head high above as a vast rock that overhung the valley.
His thumbnail﻿—widened by labour with spade and axe﻿—his thumbnail would have covered either of the tiny creatures as his shield covered Ajax.

===Chinese===
一只老鼠来到那只脚旁——那只脚穿着一只硕大的、铁锈色的带铁掌靴子，搁在围栏上，因为他的膝盖交叉着。他那满是三月尘土、被劳作弄得污迹斑斑的破旧裤腿卷得比靴筒稍高一些。那只老鼠试了好几次才爬到裤腿上，但它成功了，还大胆地爬上了艾登的膝盖。另一只紧随其后，两只老鼠就在他裤腿的褶皱里大嚼面包和奶酪的碎屑。
一只硕大的棕色手就在口袋里，离它们很近——一只巨手，在它面前，它们确实是巨人国度里的侏儒。那只手的拇指和食指若夹起一颗成熟而壳硬的核桃，它们的性命在指间又值几何？
仅凭这只手的尺寸——体积——重量，便已如一座山笼罩着它们；他宽阔的身躯如同阿尔卑斯山脉；他高高昂起的头则如一块俯瞰山谷的巨岩。
他的指甲——因使铁锹和斧头的劳作而变宽——他的指甲足以覆盖它们中的任何一只，如同他的盾覆盖了埃阿斯（Ajax）。

===Original===
Yet the little things fed in perfect confidence. He was so still, so *very* still﻿—quiescent﻿—they feared him no more than they did the wall; they could not hear his breathing.
Had they been gifted with human intelligence that very fact would have excited their suspicions. Why so very, *very* still? Strong men, wearied by work, do not sleep quietly; they breathe heavily. Even in firm sleep we move a little now and then, a limb trembles, a muscle quivers, or stretches itself.
But Iden was so still it was evident he was really wide awake and restraining his breath, and exercising conscious command over his muscles, that this scene might proceed undisturbed.

===Chinese===
然而这些小东西却在十足的安心状态中进食。他是那么安静，那么*非常*安静——静如止水——它们对他毫不畏惧，就像不怕墙一样；它们听不见他的呼吸。
假如它们拥有足以猜测的智慧，正是这一点会引起它们的怀疑。为什么如此、如此地安静？强壮的人，劳作疲惫之后，睡觉也不会安安稳稳；他们会沉重地呼吸。即便在沉睡中，我们也时不时动一下，一条腿颤一颤，一块肌肉抖一抖，或者伸一伸懒腰。
但艾登如此安静，显然他其实完全清醒，正在屏住呼吸，有意识地控制着自己的肌肉，好让这一幕不受干扰地继续下去。

===Original===
Now the strangeness of the thing was in this way: Iden set traps for mice in the cellar and the larder, and slew them there without mercy. He picked up the trap, swung it round, opening the door at the same instant, and the wretched captive was dashed to death upon the stone flags of the floor. So he hated them and persecuted them in one place, and fed them in another.
A long psychological discussion might be held on this apparent inconsistency, but I shall leave analysis to those who like it, and go on recording facts. I will only make one remark. That nothing is consistent that is human. If it was not inconsistent it would have no association with a living person.

===Chinese===
而这件事的蹊跷之处在于：艾登在地窖和食品储藏室里设置捕鼠夹，毫不留情地杀死它们。他抓起捕鼠夹，一边甩动一边同时打开门，那可怜的俘虏便被猛摔在石头地板上摔死。所以他在一个地方憎恨、迫害它们，在另一个地方却喂养它们。
关于这种表面矛盾，可以展开一场漫长的心理学讨论，但我把分析留给喜欢它的人，自己只管记录事实。我只说一点：凡是人的事，没有一件是前后一致的。如果它不是矛盾的，它就与一个活生生的人无关。

===Original===
From the merest thin slit, as it were, between his eyelids, Iden watched the mice feed and run about his knees till, having eaten every crumb, they descended his leg to the floor.

===Chinese===
艾登从他那仿佛只有一丝缝隙的眼皮之间，注视着老鼠们在他膝头吃食、跑来跑去，直到它们吃完了每一粒碎屑，顺着他的腿溜到地板上。

## IX

===Original===
This discordance between her father and mother hurt Amaryllis' affectionate heart exceedingly. It seemed to be always breaking out all the year round.

===Chinese===
父母之间的分歧深深刺痛了阿玛丽莉斯（Amaryllis）那颗多情的心。这种不和似乎整年都在不断地爆发。

===Original===
Of a summer's eve, when the day's work among the hot hay was done, Iden would often go out and sit under the russet apple till the dew had filled the grass like a green sea. When the tide of the dew had risen he would take off his heavy boots and stockings, and so walk about in the cool shadows of eve, paddling in the wet grass. He liked the refreshing coolness and the touch of the sward. It was not for washing, because he was scrupulously clean under the ragged old coat; it was because he liked the grass. There was nothing very terrible in it; men, and women, too, take off their shoes and stockings, and wade about on the sands at the sea, and no one thinks that it is anything but natural, reasonable, and pleasant. But, then, you see, *everybody* does it at the seaside, and Iden alone waded in the dew, and that was his crime—that he alone did it.

===Chinese===
夏日的傍晚，当一天在燥热的干草中劳作完毕后，艾登（Iden）常常会出门坐在那棵褐苹果树下，直到露水盈盈如一片碧绿的海洋铺满草地。待露潮涨起，他便脱去沉重的靴子和袜子，在黄昏清凉的阴影中踩着湿漉漉的草地漫步。他喜欢那沁人心脾的凉意和草皮的触感。他这样做并非为了清洗——他那件破旧外套底下其实整洁得无可挑剔——而是因为他喜欢草地。这本来算不上什么大不了的事：男人也好，女人也罢，都会脱了鞋袜在海边的沙滩上蹚水，没有人觉得这有何不妥，反倒觉得自然、合理、惬意。可是你看，*人人*都在海边这么做，唯独艾登独自在露水中蹚水，这便是他的"罪过"——唯独他一人这么干。

===Original===
